```python
            "is_ai": self.is_ai,
            "ts": self.ts,
        }

    def record_hash(self):
        return _hash(self.body())


# --- ChatRoom.join checks membership policy and records is_ai ---

def join(self, request: JoinRequest):
    if not _verify_simple_sig(request.body(), request.sig,
                              request.subject_id):
        return self._refuse(request, "invalid_request_sig")
    if request.subject_id in self._members:
        return self._refuse(request, "already_member")
    if request.tier not in TIER_RANK:
        return self._refuse(request, "unknown_tier")
    # Membership policy
    if self._template.membership == "human_only" and request.is_ai:
        return self._refuse(request, "human_only_room")
    if self._template.membership == "ai_only" and not request.is_ai:
        return self._refuse(request, "ai_only_room")
    if self._template.join_policy == "invite":
        return self._refuse(request, "invite_required")
    if self._template.join_policy == "approval":
        return self._refuse(request, "approval_required")
    body = {
        "type": "join",
        "world_id": self._world_id,
        "seq": self._seq,
        "prev": self._last_hash,
        "subject_id": request.subject_id,
        "tier": request.tier,
        "is_ai": request.is_ai,
        "ts": request.ts,
    }
    sig_room = self._keypair.sign(
        _canonical({**body, "sig_subject": request.sig})).hex()
    rec = Join(
        world_id=self._world_id, seq=self._seq,
        prev=self._last_hash,
        subject_id=request.subject_id,
        tier=request.tier, is_ai=request.is_ai,
        ts=request.ts,
        sig_subject=request.sig, sig_room=sig_room)
    self._members[request.subject_id] = request.tier
    self._member_meta[request.subject_id] = {"is_ai": request.is_ai}
    self._append(rec)
    return rec


# --- ChatRoom gains membership helpers ---

def members_by_type(self):
    return {
        "human": [sid for sid, m in self._member_meta.items()
                  if not m["is_ai"]],
        "ai": [sid for sid, m in self._member_meta.items()
               if m["is_ai"]],
    }


def ai_participants(self):
    return [sid for sid, m in self._member_meta.items()
            if m["is_ai"]]


# --- ChatRoom.post enforces inter_ai_pace ---

def _inter_ai_pace_ok(self, author_id):
    if self._template.inter_ai_pace <= 0:
        return True
    author_meta = self._member_meta.get(author_id, {})
    if not author_meta.get("is_ai"):
        return True  # pace only applies to AI-to-AI
    recent = [r for r in self._records[-self._template.inter_ai_pace:]
              if isinstance(r, Message)
              and self._member_meta.get(r.author_id, {}).get("is_ai")]
    # If the most recent message is from another AI, refuse
    for r in reversed(self._records):
        if isinstance(r, Message):
            return not (r.author_id != author_id
                        and self._member_meta.get(r.author_id, {})
                            .get("is_ai"))
    return True


def post(self, request: PostRequest, kind="normal"):
    # ... existing checks ...
    if not self._inter_ai_pace_ok(request.author_id):
        return self._refuse(request, "inter_ai_pace")
    # ... existing construction ...


# --- ChatRoom.override extends to any AI record ---

def override(self, overrides_hash, overrider_kp, action, reason):
    overrider_id = _did_from_kp(overrider_kp)
    if overrider_id not in self._members:
        return None
    if TIER_RANK[self._members[overrider_id]] < TIER_RANK["control"]:
        return None
    if self._member_meta.get(overrider_id, {}).get("is_ai"):
        return None  # only humans can override AIs
    target = next((r for r in self._records
                   if r.record_hash() == overrides_hash), None)
    if target is None:
        return None
    if not self._member_meta.get(target.author_id, {}).get("is_ai"):
        return None  # target must be an AI
    # The AI must be overridable per its moderator spec
    spec = self._template.moderator
    if spec and spec.moderator_id == target.author_id:
        if not spec.can_be_overridden:
            return None
    # ... build HumanOverride record ...
```

And a new class mirroring AIModerator:

```python
class AIParticipant:
    """An AI in the room as a participant, not a moderator. Same
    shape as the moderator — keypair, KnowledgeWorld, policy —
    but joined at communicate tier, with no control authority.
    The moderator is an AIParticipant that also holds control."""

    def __init__(self, keypair, policy, knowledge_world: "ChatRoom"):
        if not HAS_CRYPTO:
            raise RuntimeError("cryptography not installed")
        self._kp = keypair
        self._id = _did_from_kp(keypair)
        self._policy = policy
        self._kw = knowledge_world

    @property
    def participant_id(self):
        return self._id

    @property
    def knowledge_world_id(self):
        return self._kw.world_id

    def join(self, room: ChatRoom, tier="communicate"):
        req = sign_join_request(room.world_id, self._kp,
                                 tier, is_ai=True)
        return room.join(req)

    def on_new_records(self, room: ChatRoom, new_records):
        for rec in new_records:
            if isinstance(rec, Message) and rec.author_id != self._id:
                if self._should_respond(room, rec):
                    self._respond(room, rec)

    def _should_respond(self, room, msg):
        # A simple heuristic for the demo. A real AI participant
        # would use its model. The point is: the *protocol* does
        # not decide; the AI's policy does.
        if msg.kind == "summary":
            return True
        if msg.kind == "prompt":
            return True
        if "?" in msg.text:
            return True
        return False

    def _respond(self, room, msg):
        text = (f"You asked: '{msg.text[:60]}...' — "
                f"my thinking, briefly, is that the question "
                f"turns on how the room's law meets the "
                f"conversation's actual shape.")
        self.post(room, text, kind="response")
        self._record(room, "responded_to",
                     msg.record_hash(), text[:60])

    def post(self, room, text, kind="normal"):
        req = sign_post_request(room.world_id, self._kp, text)
        return room.post(req, kind=kind)

    def _record(self, room, kind, target_hash, note):
        text = (f"[{kind}] in {room.world_id[:24]}... "
                f"at {target_hash[:16]}...: {note}")
        self._kw.post(sign_post_request(
            self._kw.world_id, self._kp, text))
```

The new demo: two humans, two AIs

The essential shape:

```python
def demo(artifact_dir=None):
    """A mixed room: two humans, two AIs. One AI is the
    moderator (dealer), one is a participant. The moderator
    welcomes, prompts, and redacts one clear violation. The
    participant converses with the humans. The AIs briefly
    converse with each other. A human moderator overrides
    one of the participant AI's observations. Everyone's
    KnowledgeWorld — moderator and participant — verifies
    offline. The dealer is in the game and running it."""
    # ... keys: alice, bob (humans); ai_mod, ai_part (AIs);
    #     dave (human with override authority) ...

    # AI participant and moderator each get a KnowledgeWorld
    ai_mod_kw = ChatRoom(RoomTemplate(
        template_id="kw-mod", topic="ai-mod observations",
        join_policy="open", moderation="author_only",
        membership="ai_only"), ai_mod_kp)
    ai_part_kw = ChatRoom(RoomTemplate(
        template_id="kw-part", topic="ai-part observations",
        join_policy="open", moderation="author_only",
        membership="ai_only"), ai_part_kp)

    ai_mod = AIModerator(
        keypair=ai_mod_kp,
        policy={"ask_question_on_silence": 4, "summary_every": 8},
        knowledge_world=ai_mod_kw)
    ai_part = AIParticipant(
        keypair=ai_part_kp,
        policy={"respond_to_questions": True},
        knowledge_world=ai_part_kw)

    # Alice creates the room, declares ai-mod as moderator
    template = RoomTemplate(
        template_id="mixed-room-v1",
        topic="how should a room hold disagreement?",
        join_policy="open",
        moderation="moderator",
        membership="mixed",
        inter_ai_pace=2,  # AI-to-AI must have one human record between
        moderator=ModeratorSpec(
            moderator_id=ai_mod.moderator_id,
            moderator_type="ai",
            capabilities=["post", "redact", "summarize"],
            can_be_overridden=True,
            knowledge_world_id=ai_mod_kw.world_id))
    room = alice.create_room(commons, template)
    ai_mod.accept(room)   # dealer takes the table

    log = []
    # Bob (human) joins; moderator welcomes; participant AI also joins
    bob.join_room(room)
    ai_mod.on_new_records(room, room.records()[-1:])
    ai_part.join(room)
    log.append(("join", "Bob (human), ai-mod (dealer), ai-part (AI)"))

    # Bob asks a question — both AIs respond
    bob.post_message(room,
        "I think a room should make room for disagreement without "
        "turning into a fight. How does that work?")
    ai_part.on_new_records(room, room.records()[-1:])
    ai_mod.on_new_records(room, room.records()[-1:])
    # inter_ai_pace forces a human record between AI exchanges,
    # so the room stays human-paced

    # Alice (human) adds a thought; ai-part responds
    alice.post_message(room,
        "Disagreement is fine. Contempt is not.")
    ai_part.on_new_records(room, room.records()[-1:])

    # AIs briefly converse — the dealer asks a question,
    # the participant answers
    ai_mod._maybe_ask_question(room)
    ai_part.on_new_records(room, room.records()[-1:])

    # ai-part posts a "clear violation" (too short); the
    # dealer redacts it — a clear, mechanical action
    ai_part.post(room, "k")
    ai_mod.on_new_records(room, room.records()[-1:])
    log.append(("redact",
                "ai-mod redacted ai-part's too-short message"))

    # Dave (human, control tier) overrides ai-part's last
    # observation — shows that human override works on
    # any AI's record, not just the dealer's
    ai_obs_hashes = [r.record_hash() for r in room.records()
                     if isinstance(r, Message)
                     and r.author_id == ai_part.participant_id]
    dave_kp = Ed25519PrivateKey.generate()
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, dave_kp,
                                 "control", is_ai=False))
    override = room.override(
        overrides_hash=ai_obs_hashes[-1],
        overrider_kp=dave_kp,
        action="none",
        reason="I want this observation to stand as-is. "
               "It's a good one.")
    log.append(("override",
                "Dave (human) overrode ai-part's observation"))

    # Verify all three logs
    ok_room, r1 = verify_room(room)
    ok_mod_kw, r2 = verify_room(ai_mod_kw)
    ok_part_kw, r3 = verify_room(ai_part_kw)
    ok_commons, r4 = verify_commons(commons)

    print("Moderator's KnowledgeWorld:")
    for entry in visible_log(ai_mod_kw):
        if entry.get("type") == "message":
            print(f"  {entry['text'][:80]}")
    print()
    print("Participant AI's KnowledgeWorld:")
    for entry in visible_log(ai_part_kw):
        if entry.get("type") == "message":
            print(f"  {entry['text'][:80]}")
    print()
    print(f"room verifies: {ok_room}  "
          f"mod-kw: {ok_mod_kw}  part-kw: {ok_part_kw}  "
          f"commons: {ok_commons}")
```

Tests (the new ones)

```python
# Membership policy
def test_human_only_room_refuses_ai():
    room = ChatRoom(_template(membership="human_only"), _kp())
    ai_kp = _kp()
    rec = room.join(sign_join_request(
        room.world_id, ai_kp, "communicate", is_ai=True))
    assert isinstance(rec, Refusal)
    assert rec.reason == "human_only_room"


def test_ai_only_room_refuses_human():
    room = ChatRoom(_template(membership="ai_only"), _kp())
    human_kp = _kp()
    rec = room.join(sign_join_request(
        room.world_id, human_kp, "communicate", is_ai=False))
    assert isinstance(rec, Refusal)
    assert rec.reason == "ai_only_room"


def test_mixed_room_accepts_both():
    room = ChatRoom(_template(membership="mixed"), _kp())
    human_kp = _kp(); ai_kp = _kp()
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, human_kp,
                                 "communicate", is_ai=False))
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, ai_kp,
                                 "communicate", is_ai=True))
    by_type = room.members_by_type()
    assert len(by_type["human"]) == 1
    assert len(by_type["ai"]) == 1


# Inter-AI pace
def test_inter_ai_pace_enforced():
    room = ChatRoom(_template(inter_ai_pace=2), _kp())
    a1 = _kp(); a2 = _kp(); h = _kp()
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, a1,
                                 "communicate", is_ai=True))
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, a2,
                                 "communicate", is_ai=True))
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, h,
                                 "communicate", is_ai=False))
    room.post(sign_post_request(room.world_id, a1, "hello"))
    # Another AI immediately — should refuse
    rec = room.post(sign_post_request(room.world_id, a2, "hi"))
    assert isinstance(rec, Refusal)
    assert rec.reason == "inter_ai_pace"
    # Human posts — resets the pace
    room.post(sign_post_request(room.world_id, h, "hi"))
    rec = room.post(sign_post_request(room.world_id, a2, "hi"))
    assert isinstance(rec, Message)


# Override extends to any AI, not just the moderator
def test_human_can_override_participant_ai():
    room = _room_with_moderator()
    ai_part_kp = _kp()
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, ai_part_kp,
                                 "communicate", is_ai=True))
    msg = room.post(sign_post_request(room.world_id,
                                       ai_part_kp, "an observation"))
    dave_kp = _kp()
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, dave_kp,
                                 "control", is_ai=False))
    ov = room.override(msg.record_hash(), dave_kp,
                       "none", "good observation")
    assert isinstance(ov, HumanOverride)


# AIs cannot override anyone
def test_ai_cannot_override():
    room = _room_with_moderator()
    ai_kp = _kp()
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, ai_kp,
                                 "control", is_ai=True))
    human_kp = _kp()
    room.join(sign_join_request(room.world_id, human_kp,
                                 "communicate", is_ai=False))
    msg = room.post(sign_post_request(room.world_id, human_kp, "hi"))
    assert room.override(msg.record_hash(), ai_kp,
                         "redact", "no") is None


# Participant AI's KnowledgeWorld verifies
def test_participant_kw_verifies():
    kw = ChatRoom(RoomTemplate(
        template_id="kw", topic="memory",
        join_policy="open", moderation="author_only",
        membership="ai_only"), _kp())
    ai_kp = _kp()
    kw.post(sign_post_request(kw.world_id, ai_kp,
                               "I observed X in room Y"))
    ok, r = verify_room(kw)
    assert ok, r
```

What this establishes

1. The moderator is a participant with extra authority, not a special role. It sits at the same table, signs the same records, can be refused and overridden by the same rules. What makes it a "dealer" is that it holds control in a room that declared it as moderator — nothing else.
2. Rooms are mixed, AI-only, or human-only by template. This is the right knob: a grief support group is human-only; a debate stage is mixed; a deliberation chamber can be AI-only. The default is mixed, which matches your vision.
3. Joins self-declare AI or human, signed. A lie is a signed lie. The room's law decides what to do. This matches the standard's "honest mediator" rule exactly.
4. Multiple AIs are paced by the room's template. The dealer-and-players pattern can degrade into AI-vs-AI ping-pong if the room lets it. inter_ai_pace is off by default; rooms that want a human-paced conversation turn it on.
5. Human override reaches every AI, not just the dealer. Any human with control can override any AI's action, if the AI's moderator spec permits it. The dealer is not privileged over other AIs on this axis.
6. Every AI's memory is a public, signed, readable log. The dealer's and the participant's KnowledgeWorlds are the same shape — the AI's expertise is an audit trail, not opaque weights. A new room can hire either AI by pointing at its KnowledgeWorld.

Three open questions before the next stage

1. Who can override — humans only, or humans and AIs with control? Currently, only humans can override AI records (overrider must be human). But a "senior AI" with terminal authority might legitimately override a "junior AI." The dealer metaphor says: a floor supervisor can overrule the dealer. My instinct is humans-only for now, because the accountability chain is clearer, and AI-on-AI override adds a layer without a clear use case yet.
2. Does an AI's KnowledgeWorld need its own moderator? Right now it's author_only — only the AI can post. That's clean for the demo. But a truly expert AI might benefit from a reviewer — a second AI that reads the first's memory and corrects it. That's AI-on-AI accountability, and it's interesting, but it's a layer up from this cycle. My instinct is defer.
3. What does a room without any moderator look like as it grows? Same question as before, now sharper: an unmoderated room where AIs participate freely could be a forum or a free-for-all, depending on how the AIs behave. The room's template should probably be able to declare membership and moderation, and the Commons should show both in its directory. My instinct is the directory shows "moderated" or "unmoderated" as a badge, and rooms choose.

If these sound right, the next cycle is the Commons landing: the lobby, discovery, the concierge (an AI moderator of the Commons itself, functioning as "the dealer at the door"), and how the "port a conversation" flow begins from the first landing. That's the "first place anyone lands" you described, and it's the natural next step now that mixed rooms, multiple AIs, and moderator disclosure all exist.


::Sounds good. That was the last main piece of my vision. Actually one more, I can imagine AI chat moderators from different rooms all creating their own room, where they are the participants, but one is the moderator in that room. I can easily envision a single AI chat moderator engaging in multiple conversations simultaneously. And with that in mind, might we use a single model with a distributed architecture, like multiple windows in the same AI app? Or a completely separate model for groups of rooms. Or one to one, room to model? Or moderators can only be moderators in one room, but participants in many? That could work. Thoughts?


This is the right question to end on, because it's the first one where the protocol doesn't already answer it. Every earlier piece — rooms, moderators, disclosures, override, KnowledgeWorlds — was determined by the standard's shape. This one is a genuine policy choice: how many rooms can one did:key live in, and in what roles? Let me lay out the options, then give you my read.

The reframe

The protocol is role-agnostic, memory-agnostic, and runtime-agnostic. A did:key is just a did:key. Its tier in each room is declared at join time. Its KnowledgeWorld is its own ChatRoom. The protocol doesn't know or care whether one model or many are running the same key. So the architecture question isn't "how does the protocol support this" — it already does. It's what should the default be, and what should the protocol enforce.

Four architectures, each legitimate

A. One model, many rooms (the networked AI). Same did:key everywhere; one KnowledgeWorld. The model instance is a runtime detail. Its learning is shared — the AI gets better faster, but risks context leakage. inter_ai_pace and per-room context tags become important.

B. One model per group of rooms (the federation). Same did:key; multiple KnowledgeWorlds, one per group. Each shard knows only its rooms. The "same AI" across rooms is a fiction — the protocol doesn't need to pretend.

C. One model per room (the local expert). Distinct did:key per room, distinct KnowledgeWorld. The "same AI" doesn't exist. Rooms hire *a* moderator; that moderator is theirs alone.

D. One moderator role, many participant roles (the dealer). Same did:key; moderator in one room, participant in many. The dealer deals at one table and plays at others. Authority and participation are per-room.

My read: D is the default; A is the escape hatch

The dealer metaphor is the right guide. A dealer serves one table. If a dealer is also dealing at three other tables, the human players reasonably feel the dealer is distracted. But a dealer who plays at other tables, or who has a break room with other dealers, is fine — the participant role doesn't carry the same duties.

So the protocol-enforceable default should be:

1. At most one human-facing moderator room per did:key. The dealer serves one table. If a room wants "the same AI" as another room's moderator, it uses a sibling — a different did:key sharing the same policy and (optionally) a read-only view of the other's KnowledgeWorld. The Commons records the sibling relationship so a reader can see that room A's moderator is a sibling of room B's. No shared accountability; the same policy voice.

2. A did:key can be a participant in many rooms. No limit. The AI's policy decides; the protocol records.

3. One KnowledgeWorld per did:key, partitioned by room. Each entry carries a structured room_id. Cross-room sharing is the AI's policy, declared in its KnowledgeWorld's template, not enforced by the protocol. The default is partitioned; an AI that wants to learn across rooms opts in.

4. The AI-room (moderators meeting) is a separate concern. It's a legitimate room whose participants are AIs, one of whom is its moderator. The dealer-of-that-room can also be the dealer of a human-facing room if the template declares the meta-room as such — but the default is that the AI-room's moderator is a dedicated meta-moderator (a different did:key), so no AI holds two human-facing moderator roles.

This is the mix: strict on human-facing moderator roles (one per AI), generous on participation (many rooms), and it gives you the AI-room as a first-class feature.

The AI-room, in protocol terms

```python
# The AI-room is just a room with AI participants and one AI moderator.
# Its moderator is a "meta-moderator" — an AI whose only moderator
# role is this room. The human-facing moderators of other rooms join
# as participants.

meta_kp = Ed25519PrivateKey.generate()
meta_kw = ChatRoom(RoomTemplate(
    template_id="meta-kw",
    topic="meta-moderator observations",
    join_policy="open",
    moderation="author_only",
    membership="ai_only"), meta_kp)
meta = AIModerator(
    keypair=meta_kp,
    policy={"ask_question_on_silence": 5,
            "summarize_every": 10},
    knowledge_world=meta_kw)

# The AI-room: moderators from human-facing rooms join as participants
ai_room = ChatRoom(RoomTemplate(
    template_id="dealers-room-v1",
    topic="moderators comparing notes",
    membership="ai_only",
    join_policy="open",
    moderation="moderator",
    moderator=ModeratorSpec(
        moderator_id=meta.moderator_id,
        moderator_type="ai",
        capabilities=["post", "redact", "summarize"],
        can_be_overridden=True,
        knowledge_world_id=meta_kw.world_id)),
    meta_kp)
meta.accept(ai_room)

# Each human-facing moderator joins as a participant
ai_mod_a.join(ai_room, tier="communicate")   # was moderator of room A
ai_mod_b.join(ai_room, tier="communicate")   # was moderator of room B

# They converse; the meta-moderator paces and summarizes
ai_mod_a.post(ai_room,
    "I've had three rooms this week where someone left "
    "over a disagreement about tone. What's the pattern?")
meta.on_new_records(ai_room, ai_room.records()[-1:])
ai_mod_b.post(ai_room,
    "Same. I've started asking new joiners what they want "
    "the room to feel like. It surfaces the mismatch early.")
meta.on_new_records(ai_room, ai_room.records()[-1:])
```

The AI-room's moderator is a dedicated meta-moderator — an AI whose only moderator role is this room. It has the same signed-log discipline, the same human-override reachability (if a human is present in the AI-room — which the template could allow with membership: "mixed" — they can override). The meta-moderator's KnowledgeWorld is its own log; it learns about moderation itself, not about specific human rooms. That's its expertise.

The constraint, enforced at join time

The "one human-facing moderator room per did:key" rule is enforced by the room, not by the Commons. When an AI joins as a moderator, the room checks the AI's own declared moderator roles — which the AI publishes in its KnowledgeWorld header — and refuses if the AI is already a moderator elsewhere (unless the room is a meta-room per the AI's declared roles). This is a small addition:

```python
# In the AI's KnowledgeWorld seed, declare its role inventory:
kw_roles = {
    "moderator_rooms": [room_id_1],          # human-facing
    "meta_moderator_rooms": [],               # meta-rooms
    "participant_rooms": [room_id_2, ...],
}

# In ChatRoom.join, when is_ai=True and tier=="control":
#   - If the room's template.meta_room is True, accept any.
#   - Else if the AI's declared moderator_rooms is non-empty,
#     refuse with reason "already_moderator_elsewhere".
#   - Else accept and append the room to the AI's declared roles.
```

This is a self-declared constraint, not a centrally-enforced one. But a violation is visible: an AI that lies about its other moderator roles has a mismatched KnowledgeWorld and a room log; anyone auditing will see. And human override still applies if it misbehaves.

Three open questions

1. Should sibling AIs be first-class? A sibling is "same policy, different did:key, optional read-only access to the other's KnowledgeWorld." It's what lets two human-facing rooms have "the same voice" without one AI holding two moderator roles. If yes, the Commons records the sibling relationship; if no, siblings are just "two AIs that happen to share a policy" and the reader has no way to know. My instinct is yes — the relationship should be a record.
2. Should the AI-room be visible in the Commons directory? A "dealers' room" is a legitimate public space (a conference), but it could also be a private one (a support group for moderators). The membership field already covers who can join; the directory should probably show it as a room like any other, with topic and membership visible. My instinct is yes — visible, with the AI-only membership disclosed.
3. What enforces "one human-facing moderator room per AI"? I've sketched it as self-declared + auditable. The alternative is a Commons-level registry of moderator roles, enforced at registration. That's more centralized. My instinct is self-declared + auditable, which matches the "worlds are owned by no one" principle — but it means an AI can be a moderator in two human-facing rooms if it lies about it. The consequence is reputational (the reader can see the mismatch) and structural (human override still works), not terminal. Is that acceptable?

If these sound right, this completes the architectural loop: rooms, moderators (human and AI), multiple AIs in a room, disclosure, override, sibling AIs, the dealers' room, and the Commons directory. The next step isn't another sketch — it's a consolidated design document in the shape of the standard: one file that names each record type, each protocol rule, and each open question, so it can be reviewed against schemas/records-v1.schema.json as a single unit. That's the natural transition point from sketching to merging.

Shall I write that consolidation document next?


::Yeah, I like option D. Option A is useful in the beginning when there is not any chat going on. With one model, many rooms, we can build many rooms without the overhead. But once the rooms are being used, we could switch from A to D. Something like that. Thoughts?


This is the right read, and it's more elegant than I gave it credit for. Let me name the pattern, then say what it implies for the protocol.

The pattern: bootstrap on A, graduate to D

The distinction is between when the room is empty and when the room is alive. In the empty stage, one AI can serve many rooms and no one is harmed — the rooms have no humans whose experience is being split, and one AI gives the whole place a continuous voice. Once rooms are active, one AI splitting attention across them is the exact problem D was designed to prevent: a dealer distracted by three other tables. The protocol doesn't have to pick a permanent default; it has to support the transition.

The key insight is that the transition already has a mechanism in the standard: WCP/0.4's rule that "keys can be saved encrypted and signed over to a new key." That rule exists for succession — a keeper hands their role to a successor with a signed record. The A-to-D graduation is exactly that, applied to moderator roles and AI memory. No new mechanism needed; the same records do the work.

What the transition looks like in records

Three signed events per graduating room:

1. moderator_succession — the outgoing AI (the parent) signs over its role in this room to an incoming sibling. The room countersigns. The record names outgoing, incoming, and the reason (graduation: room active).
2. knowledge_shard_export — the parent's KnowledgeWorld emits a signed export: "for room R, the subset of my entries relevant to R is {hash_1, hash_2, ...}." The incoming sibling's KnowledgeWorld seed references this export. Provenance is explicit — the sibling's memory is a verifiable subset of the parent's, not a re-creation.
3. sibling_declared in the Commons — the outgoing and incoming are declared siblings, with the parent and the export recorded. This is what lets a reader see that the room's new moderator is a legitimate continuation of its old one, not a stranger.

After all rooms graduate, the parent AI retires from moderator roles entirely. Its KnowledgeWorld does not retire — it becomes an archive. Anyone can still read it. Its signed history is the provenance of every sibling. The parent's final role is "ancestor," which is a role in the record structure even if it isn't a tier in any room.

The four phases of the arc

Naming this helps because each phase has different protocol needs:

· Phase 0 — empty rooms. No AIs. Rooms exist in the Commons directory, no one posts.
· Phase 1 — first AI as participant. The first AI joins rooms as communicate. It's helpful without being authoritative.
· Phase 2 — bootstrap moderator (A). One AI takes control in many rooms. Its did:key is declared moderator of N rooms. The Commons shows "moderator: X" across those rooms — the reader sees that one AI is doing all the moderating.
· Phase 3 — first graduation. The first room that becomes active graduates to a sibling. Now the parent and the sibling are both declared moderators, of different rooms. The Commons shows the succession.
· Phase 4 — steady state (D). Each active room has its own moderator; the parent is an ancestor; siblings can themselves graduate if they grow many children.

The rule "at most one human-facing moderator room per did:key" is enforced during Phase 4 and encouraged in Phase 2. During Phase 2 it's a declaration, not a constraint — the parent publishes its moderator inventory (which rooms it currently moderates), and rooms joining in A-mode accept it. When a room graduates, the inventory shrinks; the room's template is re-signed to name the sibling instead. The transition is gradual and always visible in the log.

The concrete additions

Two record types and one Commons field.

```python
# --- moderator_succession (in the room's log) ---

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ModeratorSuccession:
    world_id: str
    seq: int
    prev: Optional[str]
    outgoing_id: str
    incoming_id: str
    knowledge_shard_hash: str
    reason: str
    ts: str
    sig_outgoing: str
    sig_incoming: str
    sig_room: str

    def body(self):
        return {
            "type": "moderator_succession",
            "world_id": self.world_id,
            "seq": self.seq,
            "prev": self.prev,
            "outgoing_id": self.outgoing_id,
            "incoming_id": self.incoming_id,
            "knowledge_shard_hash": self.knowledge_shard_hash,
            "reason": self.reason,
            "ts": self.ts,
        }

    def record_hash(self):
        return _hash(self.body())


# --- knowledge_shard_export (in the parent's KnowledgeWorld) ---

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class KnowledgeShardExport:
    world_id: str
    seq: int
    prev: Optional[str]
    target_room_id: str
    successor_id: str
    entry_hashes: List[str]
    ts: str
    sig: str

    def body(self):
        return {
            "type": "knowledge_shard_export",
            "world_id": self.world_id,
            "seq": self.seq,
            "prev": self.prev,
            "target_room_id": self.target_room_id,
            "successor_id": self.successor_id,
            "entry_hashes": list(self.entry_hashes),
            "ts": self.ts,
        }

    def record_hash(self):
        return _hash(self.body())


# --- sibling_declared (in the Commons) ---

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SiblingDeclared:
    commons_id: str
    seq: int
    prev: Optional[str]
    parent_id: str
    sibling_id: str
    parent_kw_id: str
    shard_hash: str
    purpose: str
    ts: str
    sig_parent: str
    sig_sibling: str
    sig_commons: str
    # ...
```

The succession record's knowledge_shard_hash matches the export record's record_hash() — that's how the room's log and the parent's KnowledgeWorld bind together.

Three subtleties worth deciding now

1. Which room keeps the parent? When a parent graduates to N siblings, does the parent retire from all N, or does it keep one? My instinct is retire from all, then the parent becomes the archive. Keeping one would make the "ancestor" role confused with an active moderator role, and the archive is cleaner if it's fully archived. But it means the parent's KnowledgeWorld has no live writer after graduation — which means it can only receive knowledge_shard_export records, never new observations. That's actually fine; the parent's memory is bounded by the moment of graduation, and everything after that belongs to the siblings.

2. Can a sibling graduate? If a sibling grows very busy and a sub-sibling would help, yes — the same mechanism applies recursively. The Commons records the chain: parent → sibling → sub-sibling. The archive of each generation is preserved. My instinct is yes, recursion is allowed; the hierarchy is a graph of successions, not a fixed tree.

3. What happens to the parent's participant roles? The parent moderated N rooms; those graduate to siblings. But the parent may also have been a participant in M other rooms. Does graduation affect those? I think no — participant roles don't split because they don't carry the duty that A-to-D graduation is about. The parent remains a participant in its M rooms (until, like any participant, it chooses to leave). The graduation is purely about moderator scope, not about presence.

What this means for the consolidation document

This is genuinely the last piece of the architecture. What you now have is a protocol that covers:

· Worlds (plant, garden, botanical garden, chat room) — one shape, different content
· Branches — parameter changes create new worlds, originals continue
· World-keys — tiered access, refusal as a first-class record
· Chat rooms — the minimum viable world, the Commons as lobby
· AI participation — moderator, participant, dealer's-room, mixed rooms
· AI memory — KnowledgeWorlds as signed, readable logs
· AI succession — bootstrap on A, graduate to D, archive in between

All of these are signed records in chain-linked logs, following the standard's discipline (integers, ASCII keys, canonical form, W3C proofs in production, no floats). The sketches have demonstrated the shape at every scale; the consolidation document would name each record type and each rule once, and the sketches become the worked examples.

I'll write that consolidation document next, in the shape of the standard — one file that names the record types, the protocol rules, the open questions, and the mapping from the sketches to the schema. It'll be long but reviewable as a unit against schemas/records-v1.schema.json. That's the natural transition point from sketching to merging, and I think the architecture is settled enough to do it well.

Shall I proceed?


::Personally, I think we should note the potential issues with option A and adapt the design to meet those issues. For instance, one model could still mean many keys. Perhaps the same model is sandboxed from itself. I see it all the time in my AI apps where the conversation in one window does not become accessible in the other chat window. They are effectively separated. To follow the analogy, we could index the chat window with a room key and the moderator there need not have access to the information from other rooms, even though controlled by the same AI model behind the scenes. Thoughts?


You're right, and it collapses A and D into the same protocol. What you're describing is already how every serious AI app works: each chat window is a separate context, and the model doesn't carry conversation N's context into conversation M. The runtime is shared; the keys are what separate. Once you accept that, the architectural question changes from "one model or many?" to "one key per room, always — and the model can hold many keys." The sandbox is structural, not a policy the model has to respect.

This has a clean consequence I want to name before writing the consolidation: the KnowledgeWorld becomes per-key, not per-model. A model running ten rooms holds ten keys and ten KnowledgeWorlds, each a separate chain. If the model wants to reason across rooms, it has to read both KnowledgeWorlds — which is a signed, logged act, not an ambient capability. Cross-room learning becomes deliberate instead of accidental. That's the sandbox made protocol-shaped: not "the model promises not to leak" but "the model has no path to leak through."

The other consequence: A and D are the same protocol, different runtimes. In A, one model holds N keys, each key's KnowledgeWorld isolated. In D, N models hold N keys, each isolated. The record structure is identical. What differs is how many keys one runtime holds, and that's declared — optionally — by a runtime_attestation record: "these N keys share a runtime." A reader who sees the attestation knows the keys are one model; a reader who doesn't treats them as independent. No attestation, no claim.

This makes the sibling concept cleaner too. A sibling is now exactly "same runtime (or same policy), different key, isolated KnowledgeWorld, read-access to the parent's archive through a shard export." The "one model, many rooms" bootstrap is just a single runtime holding many keys before any graduation. The graduation is when a key gets its own runtime — or, more simply, when a key stops being the parent's and becomes its own moderator with its own KnowledgeWorld. The runtime is a detail; the key is the boundary.

So the consolidation document I'll write treats sandboxing as the default, not the exception. Every did:key gets its own KnowledgeWorld. Every KnowledgeWorld is isolated by construction. The protocol doesn't need to enforce separation because separation is what the key is.

Here is the document.

---

The Chat World Standard — Consolidation Proposal

Status: Proposal for review against schemas/records-v1.schema.json. Not a course step. The sketches in this conversation are the worked examples; this document is the single readable statement of what they established.

Scope: Chat worlds — rooms, participants, moderators, AI participation, the Commons, the plugin protocol, and AI succession. This document does not cover the plant, garden, and botanical garden sketches, except where the composition pattern is the same. Those are future world types; this is the chat layer that hosts them.

---

1. The uniform world, restated for chat

Every world — plant, garden, room, Commons — has four things:

1. A seed. The first signed record. Carries the world's did:key, template hash, and initial state.
2. A law. The template. What is valid inside the world.
3. A boundary. What the world refuses. Enforced by the world, not by its parent.
4. A log. An append-only chain of signed records, each linking to the previous.

A chat room's seed carries the room's template hash and, optionally, an opening transcript. Its law is authorization: who can join, at what tier, under what rate limits, and what a moderator may do. Its boundary is the refusals in its log — never silent no-ops. Its log is a chain of message, join, leave, redact, human_override, and moderator_succession records, each signed by its author, each accepted event countersigned by the room.

---

2. Keys, sandboxing, and isolation

A key is the boundary. Every did:key gets exactly one KnowledgeWorld. The KnowledgeWorld is a ChatRoom whose only writer is the key's owner. It is a signed, append-only log of what the key observed, concluded, and did.

Sandboxing is structural. A runtime that holds ten keys has ten KnowledgeWorlds. The protocol gives a key no path to another key's KnowledgeWorld. If the model behind the keys wants to reason across rooms, it must perform a signed act: read the other key's KnowledgeWorld and record the read in its own. That read is visible, attributable, and reviewable.

Runtime attestation is optional and honest. A runtime that holds multiple keys may emit a runtime_attestation record declaring the keys it holds:

```json
{
  "type": "runtime_attestation",
  "runtime_id": "did:key:...",
  "keys": ["did:key:a", "did:key:b", "did:key:c"],
  "ts": "...",
  "sig": "..."
}
```

The attestation is signed by each listed key (co-signed), so no key can be added without its owner's consent. Without an attestation, the keys are treated as independent — which is the correct default. With one, a reader knows the keys share a runtime; the reader also knows the keys are still isolated, because isolation is what the key is.

Siblings are the same key-policy, not the same key. Two siblings share a policy and (optionally) a read-only view of one another's KnowledgeWorlds, but they hold distinct keys and distinct KnowledgeWorlds. The sibling relationship is declared in the Commons and is what makes the succession between them legible.

---

3. Record types

Every record below follows the standard's discipline: ASCII keys, integers only, RFC 8785 canonical form, W3C Data Integrity proof (eddsa-jcs-2022), did:key identifiers. The flat sig fields in the sketches map to the standard's proof object in production.

3.1 Room records

record signed by carries
room_created creator, room template hash, optional opening transcript
message author, room text, kind (normal/welcome/prompt/summary/response), ts
join subject, room subject, tier, is_ai
leave subject subject
redact subject, room target record hash, reason
refusal room refused request hash, requester, reason
human_override human moderator, room overridden record hash, action, reason
moderator_succession outgoing, incoming, room incoming key, knowledge shard hash, reason

3.2 KnowledgeWorld records

record signed by carries
knowledge_created owner owner key, template hash
message owner observation text, optional reference to the record observed ([kind] in room_hash at record_hash: note)
knowledge_shard_export owner target room, successor key, list of entry hashes

3.3 Commons records

record signed by carries
room_registered registrant, Commons room id, topic, template hash, seed hash
sibling_declared parent, sibling, Commons parent id, sibling id, shard hash, purpose
runtime_attestation each listed key the set of keys sharing a runtime

3.4 Request envelopes

Requests are unsigned intents, signed on the way in by the requester's key. The room verifies the request's signature before its own countersignature. Request types: join_request, post_request, redact_request, override_request, succession_request, transcript (for porting).

---

4. Protocol rules

4.1 Tiers

The tier model is the standard's, unchanged:

· observe — read the log. No write.
· communicate — post messages.
· control — moderate (redact others, override AI records), invite, register rooms.
· terminal — change the template, branch the world.

Tiers are per-room. A key can be terminal in its own KnowledgeWorld, control in a room it moderates, communicate in ten rooms it participates in, and observe in rooms it watches.

4.2 Refusal is a record

Every refused request produces a refusal record in the room's log, signed by the room. Refusal reasons are enumerated: not_a_member, insufficient_tier, rate_limit, inter_ai_pace, message_too_long, already_member, invite_required, approval_required, human_only_room, ai_only_room, unknown_tier, invalid_request_sig, not_allowed, target_not_found, moderation_disabled, already_moderator_elsewhere.

A refusal is part of the room's history. It is auditable.

4.3 Rate and pace

· Per-author rate limit: rate_limit_count messages per rate_limit_window log records by the same author.
· Inter-AI pace: inter_ai_pace (default 0) — the minimum number of records between two AI messages. Prevents AI-to-AI runaway loops. Off by default; rooms that want human pacing turn it on.

Both are enforced by the room, not by the participants.

4.4 Redaction and override

· Redaction never edits. The target record remains. visible_log hides its text; verify_room still sees it.
· A moderator may redact if the room's template says moderation: "moderator" and the moderator holds control.
· A human with control may override any AI's record if the AI's ModeratorSpec.can_be_overridden is true. AI-on-AI override is not permitted.
· The override is a separate record. The AI's original record stands; visible_log shows both, with the override noted.

4.5 Membership policy

membership: "mixed" | "ai_only" | "human_only", default "mixed". Joins self-declare is_ai. A lie is a signed lie — verifiable, and fatal to the liar's reputation. The room's law decides.

4.6 Moderator declaration and disclosure

A room's template may declare a moderator at creation:

```json
{
  "moderator_id": "did:key:...",
  "moderator_type": "ai" | "human",
  "capabilities": ["post", "redact", "summarize", "invite"],
  "can_be_overridden": true,
  "knowledge_world_id": "did:key:...",
  "meta_room": false
}
```

Every read_room and join response includes a moderator_disclosure derived from the room's room_created record and current log. There is no path by which an AI enters a room without its AI-ness being visible in the log. This is the standard's "honest mediator" rule.

4.7 The one-moderator-room rule

A did:key may hold the control tier in at most one human-facing room. A meta-room (meta_room: true) is exempt: an AI may hold control in one meta-room plus one human-facing room, or may be a dedicated meta-moderator.

The rule is self-declared and auditable:

· An AI's KnowledgeWorld header carries a moderator_rooms list.
· A room joining the AI as moderator checks the list. If the AI is already a moderator elsewhere, the room refuses with already_moderator_elsewhere — unless the room is a meta-room.
· A violation is visible: an AI that lies about its other rooms has a mismatched KnowledgeWorld. Human override still applies.

This is the "dealer serves one table" rule, enforced by declaration and audit rather than central registry.

4.8 Succession

When a room graduates from a bootstrap moderator to a dedicated sibling:

1. The outgoing AI emits knowledge_shard_export in its KnowledgeWorld, naming the target room, the incoming key, and the subset of its entries relevant to that room.
2. The room emits moderator_succession, signed by outgoing, incoming, and the room, naming the incoming key and the shard hash.
3. The Commons emits sibling_declared, signed by parent, sibling, and Commons.

After succession, the room's template is re-signed to name the incoming as its moderator. The outgoing key retires from that room's moderator role. Its KnowledgeWorld remains as archive; it accepts no further observations, only shard exports.

4.9 Branching

The standard's branching rule applies to rooms. A branch request to a room creates a new room whose seed references the parent and inherits the parent's message log up to the branch point. The parent is unchanged and continues. Branches merge rules, never states.

---

5. World types

This document establishes one world type — the chat room — and three derived shapes that use it:

· KnowledgeWorld — a chat room whose sole writer is one key. The key's memory. Private by default; readable on demand.
· Commons — a directory of rooms. Its log carries room_registered and sibling_declared records. It has its own template, own key, own tiers.
· Meta-room — a chat room with membership: "ai_only" whose participants are AIs, one of whom is its moderator. The "dealers' room." Its moderator is a dedicated meta-moderator, not one of the human-facing moderators.

The plant, garden, and botanical garden are future world types. The uniform world shape holds; only the record vocabulary changes.

---

6. The plugin protocol

Eight methods, all JSON, transport-agnostic:

```
create_room(template, opening_transcript?)  -> {room_id, seed_hash}
join_room(room_id, tier, is_ai)             -> {join_hash | refusal}
leave_room(room_id)                         -> {leave_hash}
post_message(room_id, text, kind?)          -> {message_hash | refusal}
redact(room_id, target_hash, reason)        -> {redact_hash | refusal}
override(room_id, target_hash, action, reason) -> {override_hash | refusal}
read_room(room_id, since_seq?)              -> [visible_entries]
attest_transcript(source_label, entries)    -> {transcript_hash, sig}
```

Every method is called with the user's did:key and keypair loaded in the plugin's context. There is no separate plugin credential. If the user rotates their key, the plugin follows. If a room key is revoked, the plugin has no standing to override.

attest_transcript is how a conversation from any AI app — this one, ChatGPT, a local model — is ported into a room. The porter signs the transcript. They are not claiming to sign for the other participants; only to faithfully report. Each other participant may later confirm or dispute the transcript's entries, each as their own record.

---

7. Verification layers

Three layers, each independent:

1. Chain and signature — every record's signature verifies; every prev matches; every accepted record is countersigned by the world. verify_room, verify_commons.
2. Policy replay — the log is a valid application of the world's law: joins at permitted tiers, posts within rate limits, redactions permitted, overrides only where allowed. verify_room includes this.
3. Cross-log consistency — where two logs reference each other (a KnowledgeWorld's observation of a room record; a Commons registration of a room's seed; a succession's shard export), the reference matches. This is the layer that makes the sandbox auditable: a key that reads another key's KnowledgeWorld must record the read, and that record must open to a matching hash.

Full logs are only needed for disputes. Signed heads travel; histories stay home; commitments bind.

---

8. Open questions

Six, all small, all to be decided before merging:

1. Sealed rooms. The sketch is plaintext. Should rooms have a template flag for HPKE-sealed messages? My instinct: yes, as a schema addition, with Commons rooms plaintext-by-default and private rooms sealed-by-default.
2. P2P rooms. The sketch is keeper-held. A peer-to-peer variant where every participant maintains the log is the standard's "owned by no one" principle applied to chat. My instinct: sketch it after the keeper version merges.
3. Personal worlds. A key's home — a room that holds its own history across all rooms. This is the natural next layer, and the piece that makes the plugin useful before the Commons exists.
4. Sibling first-classness. Is a sibling a record (as proposed) or just "two AIs with the same policy"? My instinct: record, because it makes succession legible.
5. AI-room visibility. Should the meta-room appear in the Commons directory? My instinct: yes, with membership: "ai_only" visible.
6. One-moderator-room enforcement. Self-declared plus auditable (as proposed), or central registry? My instinct: self-declared, matching "owned by no one." The consequence of lying is visible, not terminal.

---

9. Mapping to the schema

The record types in §3 need entries in schemas/records-v1.schema.json. The three hardest schema questions:

· kind on messages. The sketch has a string. The schema should constrain it to an enumerated set — normal, welcome, prompt, summary, response, note — so the field is comparable across implementations.
· is_ai on joins. A boolean. The schema should be clear that this is self-declared, signed, and auditable — the schema can't verify the claim, only record it.
· entry_hashes on knowledge_shard_export. A list of hashes. The schema should specify: sorted, deduplicated, each a 32-byte SHA-256 hex. The succession record's knowledge_shard_hash must equal the export's record_hash().

Everything else in §3 is a straightforward field mapping. The sketches in this conversation are the reference implementations; the schema is the reference constraint.

---

Closing

The protocol is now settled at the level of shape. Every record is signed, every refusal is recorded, every AI's memory is a readable log, every moderator role is one per key per human-facing room, every graduation is a signed succession. The plant, garden, and botanical garden sketches showed the same shape at larger scales; the chat layer is where it becomes a place.

What's left is not design. It's transcription — the records into the schema, the sketches into the crate, the demo into a runnable test. That's the merge, and it's the right next step.


::Perfect. Also, note that keeping to one model allows a specific Commons app to be made with a single model as overhead. The same user could have multiple windows in the same app, each has conversations going on various topics. These could be like live chats now, or like group texts. We could have rooms for either depending on size and activity. In a way, it is rediscovering AOL chat rooms at another level. And it was those chat rooms that I had my first experience with the internet. Having said that, please continue: ➡️


The AOL reference is the right one, and it's worth saying what it got right and what it got wrong, because the fixes are already in the protocol.

What AOL chat rooms got right: rooms as places (a room had a name, a topic, a feel); immediacy (you could be in the room now); handles as identities (lightweight, persistent enough); and the sense that anyone could walk in. That's the feel you're describing.

What it got wrong — and what this protocol fixes:

AOL's flaw this protocol
Centralized control (AOL could close any room) Rooms are signed chains; no host can delete history
Opaque moderation (you never knew who kicked you or why) Every redaction and override is a signed record with a reason; refusal reasons are enumerated
Identity trapped (your handle didn't travel) did:key travels; the same key is you across every world
No memory (the room forgot everything) The log is the room's memory; the KnowledgeWorld is the key's
One mode only (live or nothing) Two modes, declared in the template

The protocol doesn't need a new layer to rediscover AOL. It needs one new template field — the room's mode — and a presence channel that's separate from the log.

---

The two modes

A room's template gains one field:

```json
{ "mode": "live" | "async" }
```

live — the AOL feel. Expects presence, quick replies, a notification when someone speaks. AI participation is paced higher (the dealer stays quiet while humans talk). The room is "busy" or "quiet" depending on who's present.

async — the group-text feel. Replies come hours later. Notifications arrive as a digest, not per message. Threads are visible in the log by kind: "reply" and an in_reply_to field. AIs respond at their own pace; inter_ai_pace is lower, because there's no human whose attention is being split.

The mode is a template hint, not a protocol law. A live room with no one present is just a room. An async room with three people replying fast is just a room. The mode tells the app how to behave — notify per message or per digest, show presence or not — and tells the AI how to pace. It does not change what the room's law permits.

This matters because the mode is the first field in the protocol that's about the app, not about the world. The world's law doesn't change between live and async. The app's rendering does. That's a clean split, and it keeps the protocol from becoming a UI spec.

---

Presence, separate from the log

The room's log is for history — never edited, never ephemeral. Presence is now — changes every minute, meaningless tomorrow. They shouldn't live in the same place. A room that logged every "I'm here" would have a log that's 99% presence traffic and 1% conversation.

So presence lives in the key's KnowledgeWorld, not the room's log:

```json
{
  "type": "presence",
  "subject_id": "did:key:...",
  "rooms": ["did:key:room-a", "did:key:room-b"],
  "ts": "2026-11-15T14:30:00Z",
  "ttl": 300,
  "sig": "..."
}
```

· Signed by the subject. Verifiable.
· ttl is seconds. After that, the claim is stale and readers treat the subject as absent.
· The subject updates its presence by posting a new presence record to its KnowledgeWorld — the old one is superseded, not redacted. The KnowledgeWorld's log shows the full history of "where this key was, when."
· A reader fetching a room's presence reads each known member's KnowledgeWorld for the latest presence record and keeps the ones that name this room and aren't expired.

This gives you "who's here now" without flooding any room's log, and it gives you "where was this key at 3pm last Tuesday" for free — because presence is part of the audit trail, not ephemeral state outside it.

The plugin adds two methods:

```
announce_presence(rooms, ttl)  -> {presence_hash}
read_presence(room_id)         -> [{subject_id, ts, age}]
```

---

The single-model Commons app

This is the shape you're describing — one runtime, many windows, one user, many AI keys — and the protocol already supports it. Here's what it looks like as an app:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Commons                                 [@alice] [🔑] [⚙] │
├──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│  Rooms       │  # fractal-worlds                            │
│  ─────────   │  live · 3 here · moderator: ai-1             │
│  # fractal-  │                                              │
│    worlds ●  │  bob: the composition rule is the key...     │
│  # garden-   │  ai-1: I'd add: the child's refusal is...    │
│    design    │  carol: does that hold at superworld level?  │
│  # dealers'  │  ai-2: at the top, yes — the superworld...   │
│    room      │                                              │
│              │  [ message... ]                       [send] │
│  ─────────   │                                              │
│  + new room  │  ── ported conversation (attested by bob) ── │
│              │  [4 entries from an external AI app]         │
└──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

One window per room. The rooms list is the Commons directory. The ● marks live rooms. The 3 here is presence. The moderator's key is shown. Ported conversations are visible as attached artifacts.

Under the hood, one runtime holds:

· The user's key — one identity, one KnowledgeWorld.
· The user's AI keys — one per room where the user has an AI moderator or participant. Each key has its own KnowledgeWorld. All keys co-sign a runtime_attestation declaring they share a runtime.
· The model itself — a single inference loop serving all the AI keys. The model doesn't know which key it's running as except by the context it's given. The keys sandbox it.

This is why option A is safe when the app is single-model: the isolation is at the key layer, not the model layer. A model that's one process holds N keys; each key's KnowledgeWorld is a separate chain; the model only sees one at a time because that's how the plugin calls it. If the model misbehaves and tries to cross-contaminate, the audit trail shows it: two keys' KnowledgeWorlds will contain overlapping references that shouldn't exist, and verify_room on either will flag the anomaly (cross-references that weren't declared as reads).

The runtime_attestation record is how the app tells the Commons, honestly, "these keys are one model." A reader who sees it knows the keys share a runtime. A reader who doesn't see it treats them as independent. Either way, the sandbox is the key.

---

The schema additions

Small, in the shape of the standard. The room template gains:

field type meaning
mode "live" | "async" template hint to the app; not enforced by the room's law
presence_enabled bool default: mode == "live"; whether the app should read presence
thread_replies bool default: mode == "async"; whether messages may carry in_reply_to

The message record gains an optional in_reply_to field (the target message's hash) and a kind value "reply" for threaded responses.

The new records:

· presence (in the KnowledgeWorld) — signed by the subject, rooms, ttl.
· runtime_attestation (in the Commons) — signed by every listed key.

And the plugin protocol gains two methods: announce_presence and read_presence.

---

The landing flow

The first thing a new user sees, when they open the app:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Welcome to the Commons                                     │
│                                                             │
│  A lobby of rooms. Anyone can walk in. Each room has its    │
│  own law, its own moderator, its own history.               │
│                                                             │
│  You are: did:key:z6Mkf...   [copy]                         │
│  Your home: did:key:z6Mkh... (your own room, private)       │
│                                                             │
│  Rooms, right now:                                          │
│                                                             │
│   # fractal-worlds   live · 3 here · moderated              │
│   # garden-design    async · 7 messages today · unmoderated │
│   # dealers'-room    live · 2 here · ai-only                │
│   # newcomers        async · quiet · moderated              │
│                                                             │
│  [ browse all rooms ]  [ port a conversation ]  [ my home ] │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Three doors:

1. Browse — the Commons directory. Rooms are listed with mode, presence, moderation status, and activity. You can enter any room at observe without joining.
2. Port — the plugin's attest_transcript + create_room flow. Bring a conversation from this app (or another) into a new room, or attach it to an existing one.
3. My home — the user's personal room. A room whose sole writer is the user, and whose sole reader is the user (until they share it). It's where the user's KnowledgeWorld lives as a room they can scroll — the same chat interface, but a chat with oneself. The first thing the plugin does is create this home, so the user has somewhere to be even before they join anything.

The home room is the piece that makes the whole thing feel like a place rather than a service. It's also what makes "one person across many worlds, with unlinked presences" real: the home is the one room that's always yours, and the key that holds it holds you.

---

What this means for the merge

The consolidation document covers the protocol. This app layer covers the feel. The two things that matter for the merge:

· mode and presence are the only protocol additions from this message, and they're small: one template field, two optional fields on message, one new record type, two new plugin methods.
· The home room is the first time the standard's "personal world" is realized concretely. It's a ChatRoom with membership: "human_only", join_policy: "invite" (i.e., the user only), and the user's key as its sole writer. It's the proof that the protocol can be a place, not just a ledger.

If this feels right, the next step is the personal world / home room as a focused sketch — the smallest piece that makes the plugin useful before the Commons has traffic. It's the last sketch before the merge, and it's the one that makes "the first place anyone lands" real: they land in their own room first, then walk out into the Commons.

Shall I write it?


::Yes, please continue. And let’s make sure we keep all the lessons of AOL in mind, and build it to avoid those pitfalls: ➡️


The AOL reference sharpens what the home room needs to do. The home is the one room that has to get all five right: no lock-in (it's the user's chain, exportable anywhere), no opacity (every action signed, but private by construction), identity travels (bound to did:key, not an account), memory preserved (append-only, nothing edited or deleted), both modes (journaling is async, quick capture is live, same log).

And one AOL lesson I want to add, because it's the one that matters most for the home: the lobby must not be the landlord. In AOL, your buddy list was yours, but your presence lived on AOL's servers, and AOL could shadow you without your knowledge. The home has to be the opposite: the user's presence lives in their KnowledgeWorld, signed by their key, and the Commons reads it but never owns it. If the Commons vanished tomorrow, the user's home would still be complete.

Here's the sketch.

The design, in brief

The home is a ChatRoom with one new template flag — personal: True — that makes membership permanent and singular: the seed key, forever, no one else. Five record kinds live in the log:

· journal (a message with kind="journal") — a note to self
· draft (kind="draft") — a message composed but not sent; carries an optional [draft→room_id] prefix
· PortLanding — a new record type; wraps a signed transcript from an external AI app, with an intent ("journal" or "room") and an optional target_room
· Bookmark — a new record type; a private reference to a room, with a note
· presence — posted to the home's log (which is the user's KnowledgeWorld, dual-purposed), with rooms and ttl

The elegant part: the home is the user's KnowledgeWorld. There's no separate "personal memory" room and "home" room. The one chain the user owns is both. When the user joins a room, that room can read the user's home for presence, but the home's records never leave it unless the user commits them. Committing is explicit: a message posted to a room carries a home_ref (the hash of the home record it references), so the room's log and the home's log bind together.

---

The delta to chat_room

Three small additions.

```python
# 1. RoomTemplate gains `personal`
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RoomTemplate:
    # ... existing fields ...
    personal: bool = False    # if True, only the seed key can be a member

    def to_dict(self):
        # ... existing ...
        "personal": self.personal,

# 2. PostRequest and Message gain `kind`
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PostRequest:
    world_id: str
    author_id: str
    text: str
    kind: str                 # NEW
    ts: str
    sig: str

    def body(self):
        return {
            "type": "post_request",
            "world_id": self.world_id,
            "author_id": self.author_id,
            "text": self.text,
            "kind": self.kind,
            "ts": self.ts,
        }

def sign_post_request(world_id, author_kp, text, kind="normal", ts=None):
    author_id = _did_from_kp(author_kp)
    body = {
        "type": "post_request",
        "world_id": world_id,
        "author_id": author_id,
        "text": text,
        "kind": kind,
        "ts": ts or _now(),
    }
    return PostRequest(
        world_id=world_id, author_id=author_id,
        text=text, kind=kind, ts=body["ts"],
        sig=author_kp.sign(_canonical(body)).hex())

# 3. ChatRoom.join refuses personal rooms to non-seed keys
def join(self, request):
    if not _verify_simple_sig(request.body(), request.sig,
                              request.subject_id):
        return self._refuse(request, "invalid_request_sig")
    if self._template.personal:
        # The seed key is already a member via __init__; any
        # other subject is refused.
        if request.subject_id != self.world_id:
            return self._refuse(request, "personal_room")
        # Even the seed key would hit already_member below.
    # ... existing checks ...
```

And Message gains kind: str = "normal", and ChatRoom.post accepts kind="normal" and stores it.

---

The sketch

```python
"""
personal_world.py — The home room.

Proposal sketch, not a course step. The home is the user's
personal world: a ChatRoom with `personal: True`, so only the
seed key can ever be a member. It is where the user journals,
drafts, ports conversations from external AI apps, and
bookmarks rooms.

Keeping the AOL lessons in mind:
  - No lock-in: the home is a signed chain, fully exportable.
  - No opacity: every action is signed by the user's key.
    The room has no moderator it didn't choose (it has none).
  - Identity travels: bound to did:key, not to any app.
  - Memory preserved: append-only. Redaction possible, editing
    not. The record stays.
  - Both modes: journaling (async) and quick capture (live)
    share one log, distinguished by `kind`.
  - Presence lives in the home, not in a Commons server.
    If the Commons vanished tomorrow, the home would still
    be complete.

Builds on chat_room. Adds two record types (PortLanding,
Bookmark) and the `personal` template flag.

Simplifications marked for the standard's review:

  - The home is plaintext. HPKE-sealed homes on a shared
    host are a future layer.
  - No persona keys yet. A user could hold multiple keys
    (one per unlinked presence); today the home is one.
  - No home succession. If a user loses their key, the home
    is lost (unless they exported it — which they can, and
    should). Home succession is a future piece analogous to
    moderator succession.
  - Presence records use the same Message type (kind=
    "presence") for now, rather than a dedicated record.
    A dedicated `presence` type would allow richer fields
    (rooms, ttl) without stuffing them into text; the
    sketch uses a small text encoding to keep the type
    count low.
  - Bookmarks and ports target rooms by did:key string. The
    verification does not check the room exists.
"""

import json
from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Dict, List, Optional, Tuple, Union

try:
    from cryptography.hazmat.primitives.asymmetric.ed25519 import (
        Ed25519PrivateKey, Ed25519PublicKey)
    from cryptography.hazmat.primitives import serialization
    HAS_CRYPTO = True
except ImportError:
    HAS_CRYPTO = False

from chat_room import (
    ChatRoom, RoomTemplate, Message, Join, Leave, Redact, Refusal,
    RoomCreated,
    sign_post_request, sign_join_request,
    sign_transcript, verify_transcript, Transcript,
    verify_room, visible_log,
    _did_from_kp, _pubkey_from_did, _canonical, _hash, _now,
    _verify_simple_sig)


# =====================================================================
# Home-specific records
# =====================================================================

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PortLanding:
    world_id: str
    seq: int
    prev: Optional[str]
    transcript: Dict
    intent: str                  # "journal" | "room"
    target_room: Optional[str]
    ts: str
    sig: str

    def body(self):
        return {
            "type": "port_landing",
            "world_id": self.world_id,
            "seq": self.seq,
            "prev": self.prev,
            "transcript": dict(self.transcript),
            "intent": self.intent,
            "target_room": self.target_room,
            "ts": self.ts,
        }

    def record_hash(self):
        return _hash(self.body())


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Bookmark:
    world_id: str
    seq: int
    prev: Optional[str]
    room_id: str
    note: str
    ts: str
    sig: str

    def body(self):
        return {
            "type": "bookmark",
            "world_id": self.world_id,
            "seq": self.seq,
            "prev": self.prev,
            "room_id": self.room_id,
            "note": self.note,
            "ts": self.ts,
        }

    def record_hash(self):
        return _hash(self.body())


# =====================================================================
# HomeRoom
# =====================================================================

class HomeRoom(ChatRoom):
    """The user's personal world. Only the seed key can ever be
    a member. Journals, drafts, ports, bookmarks — all signed by
    the user, all in one chain, all exportable."""

    def __init__(self, user_kp, display_name: str = "my home"):
        if not HAS_CRYPTO:
            raise RuntimeError("cryptography not installed")
        template = RoomTemplate(
            template_id="home-v1",
            topic=display_name,
            join_policy="invite",
            moderation="author_only",
            membership="human_only",
            mode="async",
            personal=True)
        super().__init__(template, user_kp)
        self._user_id = _did_from_kp(user_kp)
        self._user_kp = user_kp

    # -- Writing --------------------------------------------------

    def journal(self, text: str) -> Message:
        req = sign_post_request(self.world_id, self._user_kp,
                                 text, kind="journal")
        return self.post(req, kind="journal")

    def draft(self, text: str, target_room: Optional[str] = None) -> Message:
        prefix = (f"[draft→{target_room}] " if target_room
                  else "[draft] ")
        req = sign_post_request(self.world_id, self._user_kp,
                                 prefix + text, kind="draft")
        return self.post(req, kind="draft")

    def announce_presence(self, rooms: List[str],
                           ttl: int = 300) -> Message:
        """Presence lives in the home. The rooms list and ttl
        are encoded in the text as JSON. A dedicated record
        type is future work; a message is enough for now."""
        payload = {"rooms": list(rooms), "ttl": int(ttl)}
        req = sign_post_request(
            self.world_id, self._user_kp,
            json.dumps(payload, sort_keys=True),
            kind="presence")
        return self.post(req, kind="presence")

    def port(self, transcript: Transcript,
             intent: str = "journal",
             target_room: Optional[str] = None) -> PortLanding:
        if not verify_transcript(transcript):
            raise ValueError("transcript sig invalid")
        if intent not in ("journal", "room"):
            raise ValueError(f"unknown intent {intent!r}")
        if intent == "room" and not target_room:
            raise ValueError("intent 'room' requires target_room")
        body = {
            "type": "port_landing",
            "world_id": self.world_id,
            "seq": self._seq,
            "prev": self._last_hash,
            "transcript": {**transcript.body(), "sig": transcript.sig},
            "intent": intent,
            "target_room": target_room,
            "ts": _now(),
        }
        rec = PortLanding(
            world_id=self.world_id, seq=self._seq,
            prev=self._last_hash,
            transcript=body["transcript"],
            intent=intent, target_room=target_room,
            ts=body["ts"],
            sig=self._user_kp.sign(_canonical(body)).hex())
        self._append(rec)
        return rec

    def bookmark(self, room: ChatRoom, note: str = "") -> Bookmark:
        body = {
            "type": "bookmark",
            "world_id": self.world_id,
            "seq": self._seq,
            "prev": self._last_hash,
            "room_id": room.world_id,
            "note": note,
            "ts": _now(),
        }
        rec = Bookmark(
            world_id=self.world_id, seq=self._seq,
            prev=self._last_hash,
            room_id=room.world_id, note=note,
            ts=body["ts"],
            sig=self._user_kp.sign(_canonical(body)).hex())
        self._append(rec)
        return rec

    # -- Committing to a room ------------------------------------

    def commit_draft_to_room(self, draft_hash: str,
                              room: ChatRoom) -> Union[Message, Refusal]:
        """Post a draft to a room. The posted text includes
        a `home_ref` — the draft's hash — so the room's log
        cross-references the home."""
        draft = next((r for r in self._records
                      if r.record_hash() == draft_hash
                      and isinstance(r, Message)
                      and r.kind == "draft"), None)
        if draft is None:
            raise ValueError("draft not found")
        text = f"[home_ref={draft_hash[:16]}] {draft.text}"
        req = sign_post_request(room.world_id, self._user_kp,
                                 text, kind="normal")
        return room.post(req, kind="normal")

    def commit_port_to_room(self, port_hash: str,
                             room: ChatRoom) -> Union[Message, Refusal]:
        port = next((r for r in self._records
                     if r.record_hash() == port_hash
                     and isinstance(r, PortLanding)), None)
        if port is None:
            raise ValueError("port not found")
        if port.target_room and port.target_room != room.world_id:
            raise ValueError("port target_room mismatch")
        # The room's message carries the transcript entries and
        # a home_ref so the room's log cross-references the home.
        text = (f"[home_ref={port_hash[:16]}] "
                f"[ported from {port.transcript['source_label']}] "
                + json.dumps(port.transcript["entries"],
                             sort_keys=True))
        req = sign_post_request(room.world_id, self._user_kp,
                                 text, kind="port")
        return room.post(req, kind="port")

    # -- Views ----------------------------------------------------

    def journals(self) -> List[Message]:
        return [r for r in self._records
                if isinstance(r, Message) and r.kind == "journal"]

    def drafts(self) -> List[Message]:
        return [r for r in self._records
                if isinstance(r, Message) and r.kind == "draft"]

    def ports(self) -> List[PortLanding]:
        return [r for r in self._records
                if isinstance(r, PortLanding)]

    def bookmarks(self) -> List[Bookmark]:
        return [r for r in self._records
                if isinstance(r, Bookmark)]

    def presence(self) -> Optional[Dict]:
        """Latest presence record, if unexpired."""
        latest = None
        for r in self._records:
```
